Yeah, I have. It’s not dramatic, but things that used to feel kind of clean and step-by-step now take me longer. Yeah… I do feel worn down a lot. Even if I sleep through the night, I don’t really wake up feeling refreshed. It’s more like I’m starting the day already partly used up.

And then by the time I’ve gone to work and dealt with the normal house stuff—just clutter, dishes, things sitting where they shouldn’t be—I don’t have much left. It’s funny, because sometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should clear off that table,” and then I end up noticing some random stack of papers from months ago, and suddenly it feels like this whole archaeological dig in my own house.

But, yeah, the main thing is I’m tired most of the time. Not completely unable to function, but just… worn down.

I don’t know, my mind just feels slower, and I second-guess small things more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like baking or using your telescope?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I still sort of… do them sometimes, or I think about doing them. But it doesn’t feel the same. Baking used to feel like a nice little project, like there was some comfort in it, and the telescope thing—checking the sky, seeing if it was clear—that used to give me this little bit of excitement.\n\nNow most days there’s just this low, gray feeling sitting in the background. I can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through things, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things the way I used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level or do you feel more fatigued than usual, even after resting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel worn down a lot. Even if I sleep through the night, I don’t really wake up feeling refreshed. It’s more like I’m starting the day already partly used up.\n\nAnd then by the time I’ve gone to work and dealt with the normal house stuff—just clutter, dishes, things sitting where they shouldn’t be—I don’t have much left. It’s funny, because sometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should clear off that table,” and then I end up noticing some random stack of papers from months ago, and suddenly it feels like this whole archaeological dig in my own house.\n\nBut, yeah, the main thing is I’m tired most of the time. Not completely unable to function, but just… worn down.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through things, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things the way I used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level or do you feel more fatigued than usual, even after resting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel worn down a lot. Even if I sleep through the night, I don’t really wake up feeling refreshed. It’s more like I’m starting the day already partly used up.\n\nAnd then by the time I’ve gone to work and dealt with the normal house stuff—just clutter, dishes, things sitting where they shouldn’t be—I don’t have much left. It’s funny, because sometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should clear off that table,” and then I end up noticing some random stack of papers from months ago, and suddenly it feels like this whole archaeological dig in my own house.\n\nBut, yeah, the main thing is I’m tired most of the time. Not completely unable to function, but just… worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… not really that I’ve noticed. My appetite kind of varies day to day, but I don’t think there’s been some big change.\n\nWeight-wise, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. I mean, I have a scale somewhere, but it’s probably under a pile of things in the bathroom closet, which is… its own situation. But my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think anything significant has happened there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty about things than usual, or like you’re not measuring up in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in some big, dramatic way, I don’t think. I mean, I have the usual kind of “ugh, I should be doing more” feeling, especially when I see things piling up or I think about stuff I meant to finish.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s guilt exactly. Sometimes it’s more like this vague sense that everyone else has a cleaner system for being a person, if that makes sense. Like they have their calendar and their meal plan and their laundry somehow not becoming a geological formation.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess… I haven’t really noticed feeling unusually guilty, no. More just frustrated with myself sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still more often lately?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed myself moving especially slowly, at least not in a way someone else would point out.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to tell because I’ll sit down to do one thing and then remember three other things, like I’ll get up to check the laundry and end up reorganizing one shelf for no good reason. But that’s kind of just… how my house stuff goes.\n\nSo I guess no, not really a clear change like that. I haven’t been thinking, “Wow, I can’t sit still,” or “I’m moving really slow.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of making plans to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I get kind of tired of everything being such a slog, like even small things feel weirdly complicated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been thinking life isn’t worth living. It’s more like… I wish I could hit pause for a while, if that makes sense. Like when my laptop has too many tabs open and the fan starts making that little airplane noise—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nBut no, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I think so. Not like a dramatic “nothing will ever get better” thing, but I do catch myself assuming the house is just never going to really be settled. Like there will always be another repair, another half-finished corner, some random draft I can’t track down.

And then that kind of turns into, “Maybe this is just how I function now,” like I’m going to keep running at this reduced level. Which sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it does make me avoid planning things ahead. Even stuff I used to like, like trips or little stargazing projects—I’ll start thinking about equipment or weather or where to go, and then I just sort of decide it probably won’t happen anyway.

Hmm… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed my speech being slower, at least not in a way where someone has pointed it out. I do sometimes lose my train of thought, but that’s different, I think.

It’s not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, exactly. I still go to the lab, I talk to people, I do what I’m supposed to do. But most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s weird, because I used to get a little spark from things—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing if the sky was clear enough—and now it’s more like… I do them, or I think about doing them, but it doesn’t feel light the way it used to. It’s just sort of there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It’s not dramatic, but things that used to feel kind of clean and step-by-step now take me longer. Like revising a paragraph for a grant—I used to be able to just move through it, fix the structure, tighten it up. Now I’ll stare at one sentence and change a word back and forth, then wonder if the whole point is wrong.\n\nAnd even little decisions, like picking materials for something at home—there was a repair I was trying to plan, and I kept comparing options way longer than I needed to. I don’t know, my mind just feels slower, and I second-guess small things more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like baking or using your telescope?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I still sort of… do them sometimes, or I think about doing them. But it doesn’t feel the same. Baking used to feel like a nice little project, like there was some comfort in it, and the telescope thing—checking the sky, seeing if it was clear—that used to give me this little bit of excitement.\n\nNow most days there’s just this low, gray feeling sitting in the background. I can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through things, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things the way I used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level or do you feel more fatigued than usual, even after resting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel worn down a lot. Even if I sleep through the night, I don’t really wake up feeling refreshed. It’s more like I’m starting the day already partly used up.\n\nAnd then by the time I’ve gone to work and dealt with the normal house stuff—just clutter, dishes, things sitting where they shouldn’t be—I don’t have much left. It’s funny, because sometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should clear off that table,” and then I end up noticing some random stack of papers from months ago, and suddenly it feels like this whole archaeological dig in my own house.\n\nBut, yeah, the main thing is I’m tired most of the time. Not completely unable to function, but just… worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… not really that I’ve noticed. My appetite kind of varies day to day, but I don’t think there’s been some big change.\n\nWeight-wise, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. I mean, I have a scale somewhere, but it’s probably under a pile of things in the bathroom closet, which is… its own situation. But my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think anything significant has happened there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty about things than usual, or like you’re not measuring up in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in some big, dramatic way, I don’t think. I mean, I have the usual kind of “ugh, I should be doing more” feeling, especially when I see things piling up or I think about stuff I meant to finish.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s guilt exactly. Sometimes it’s more like this vague sense that everyone else has a cleaner system for being a person, if that makes sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not completely, I guess. It’s more like they’ve… faded out.

I still get my lab work done, which is funny because at work if there’s a procedure and samples waiting, I can sort of just follow the track. But the grant drafts or even normal project decisions take me way longer than they should. I’ll open the document and then somehow spend twenty minutes reorganizing folders or checking some detail that probably doesn’t matter.

At home, though, yeah, the baking and the bonsai stuff and the telescope plans have mostly stopped being part of my week. I keep thinking I’ll get back to them—like I still have the flour containers and the little pruning shears, and the telescope is literally right there—but I don’t plan around them anymore the way I used to. They used to kind of structure my downtime, and now I just… don’t follow through.

It’s not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, exactly. I still go to the lab, I talk to people, I do what I’m supposed to do. But most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s weird, because I used to get a little spark from things—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing if the sky was clear enough—and now it’s more like… I do them, or I think about doing them, but it doesn’t feel light the way it used to. It’s just sort of there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It’s not dramatic, but things that used to feel kind of clean and step-by-step now take me longer. Now I’ll stare at one sentence and change a word back and forth, then wonder if the whole point is wrong.\n\nAnd even little decisions, like picking materials for something at home—there was a repair I was trying to plan, and I kept comparing options way longer than I needed to. I don’t know, my mind just feels slower, and I second-guess small things more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like baking or using your telescope?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I still sort of… do them sometimes, or I think about doing them. But it doesn’t feel the same. Baking used to feel like a nice little project, like there was some comfort in it, and the telescope thing—checking the sky, seeing if it was clear—that used to give me this little bit of excitement.\n\nNow most days there’s just this low, gray feeling sitting in the background. I can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through things, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things the way I used to. Even if I sleep through the night, I don’t really wake up feeling refreshed. My appetite kind of varies day to day, but I don’t think there’s been some big change.\n\nWeight-wise, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. Sometimes it’s more like this vague sense that everyone else has a cleaner system for being a person, if that makes sense. Like they have their calendar and their meal plan and their laundry somehow not becoming a geological formation.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess… I haven’t really noticed feeling unusually guilty, no. More just frustrated with myself sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still more often lately?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed myself moving especially slowly, at least not in a way someone else would point out.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to tell because I’ll sit down to do one thing and then remember three other things, like I’ll get up to check the laundry and end up reorganizing one shelf for no good reason. But that’s kind of just… how my house stuff goes.\n\nSo I guess no, not really a clear change like that. I haven’t been thinking, “Wow, I can’t sit still,” or “I’m moving really slow.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of making plans to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I get kind of tired of everything being such a slog, like even small things feel weirdly complicated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been thinking life isn’t worth living. It’s more like… I wish I could hit pause for a while, if that makes sense. Like when my laptop has too many tabs open and the fan starts making that little airplane noise—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nBut no, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this low, gray feeling, fatigue, and loss of enjoyment—in most days?\n\nPatient: I think… a little over two years? I haven’t really noticed my speech being slower, at least not in a way where someone has pointed it out. I do sometimes lose my train of thought, but that’s different, I think.\n\nAs far as fidgety… I mean, I’ll mess with my sleeves or move things around on my desk, but I’ve kind of always done that. Like if there’s a pen nearby I’ll click it without realizing, which apparently is annoying—my coworker once gave me one of those silent fidget things as a joke, or maybe not totally as a joke.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change like moving slower or being unusually restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped doing any of the activities you used to enjoy, like baking or stargazing, because they no longer feel worthwhile or you don’t expect to get anything out of them?\n\nPatient: Not completely, I guess. It’s more like they’ve… faded out.\n\nI still get my lab work done, which is funny because at work if there’s a procedure and samples waiting, I can sort of just follow the track. But the grant drafts or even normal project decisions take me way longer than they should. I’ll open the document and then somehow spend twenty minutes reorganizing folders or checking some detail that probably doesn’t matter.\n\nAt home, though, yeah, the baking and the bonsai stuff and the telescope plans have mostly stopped being part of my week. Can you tell me what’s been troubling you or what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I just feel worn down almost all the time. Even if I sleep a full night, I wake up feeling like I’m already behind, if that makes sense. I get through work, and then there’s the house clutter and all the little things piling up—mail, dishes, laundry, I don’t know why it all multiplies—and by the time I deal with that, I don’t really have much left for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, exactly. I still go to the lab, I talk to people, I do what I’m supposed to do. But most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s weird, because I used to get a little spark from things—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing if the sky was clear enough—and now it’s more like… I do them, or I think about doing them, but it doesn’t feel light the way it used to. It’s just sort of there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It’s not dramatic, but things that used to feel kind of clean and step-by-step now take me longer. Like revising a paragraph for a grant—I used to be able to just move through it, fix the structure, tighten it up. Now I’ll stare at one sentence and change a word back and forth, then wonder if the whole point is wrong.\n\nAnd even little decisions, like picking materials for something at home—there was a repair I was trying to plan, and I kept comparing options way longer than I needed to. I don’t know, my mind just feels slower, and I second-guess small things more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like baking or using your telescope?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I still sort of… do them sometimes, or I think about doing them. But it doesn’t feel the same. Baking used to feel like a nice little project, like there was some comfort in it, and the telescope thing—checking the sky, seeing if it was clear—that used to give me this little bit of excitement.\n\nNow most days there’s just this low, gray feeling sitting in the background. I can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through things, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things the way I used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level or do you feel more fatigued than usual, even after resting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel worn down a lot. Even if I sleep through the night, I don’t really wake up feeling refreshed. It’s more like I’m starting the day already partly used up.\n\nAnd then by the time I’ve gone to work and dealt with the normal house stuff—just clutter, dishes, things sitting where they shouldn’t be—I don’t have much left. It’s funny, because sometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should clear off that table,” and then I end up noticing some random stack of papers from months ago, and suddenly it feels like this whole archaeological dig in my own house.\n\nBut, yeah, the main thing is I’m tired most of the time. Not completely unable to function, but just… worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… not really that I’ve noticed. My appetite kind of varies day to day, but I don’t think there’s been some big change.\n\nWeight-wise, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. I mean, I have a scale somewhere, but it’s probably under a pile of things in the bathroom closet, which is… its own situation. But my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think anything significant has happened there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty about things than usual, or like you’re not measuring up in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in some big, dramatic way, I don’t think. I mean, I have the usual kind of “ugh, I should be doing more” feeling, especially when I see things piling up or I think about stuff I meant to finish.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s guilt exactly. Sometimes it’s more like this vague sense that everyone else has a cleaner system for being a person, if that makes sense. Like they have their calendar and their meal plan and their laundry somehow not becoming a geological formation.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess… I haven’t really noticed feeling unusually guilty, no. More just frustrated with myself sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still more often lately?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed myself moving especially slowly, at least not in a way someone else would point out.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to tell because I’ll sit down to do one thing and then remember three other things, like I’ll get up to check the laundry and end up reorganizing one shelf for no good reason. But that’s kind of just… how my house stuff goes.\n\nSo I guess no, not really a clear change like that. I haven’t been thinking, “Wow, I can’t sit still,” or “I’m moving really slow.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of making plans to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I get kind of tired of everything being such a slog, like even small things feel weirdly complicated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been thinking life isn’t worth living. It’s more like… I wish I could hit pause for a while, if that makes sense. Like when my laptop has too many tabs open and the fan starts making that little airplane noise—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nBut no, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this low, gray feeling, fatigue, and loss of enjoyment—in most days?\n\nPatient: I think… a little over two years? It kind of started after we moved into the older house. Not immediately like flipping a switch, but shortly after that, things just started feeling more gray as the default.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the house, honestly—old wiring, weird drafts, always some repair project, and I’d get distracted by one thing after another. But looking back, that’s around when this low baseline really settled in. Most days since then, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling more hopeless about the future, or like you're not good enough, more often lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like a dramatic “nothing will ever get better” thing, but I do catch myself assuming the house is just never going to really be settled. Like there will always be another repair, another half-finished corner, some random draft I can’t track down.\n\nAnd then that kind of turns into, “Maybe this is just how I function now,” like I’m going to keep running at this reduced level. Which sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it does make me avoid planning things ahead. Even stuff I used to like, like trips or little stargazing projects—I’ll start thinking about equipment or weather or where to go, and then I just sort of decide it probably won’t happen anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you're more fidgety or restless than usual, even when you're not particularly anxious?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed my speech being slower, at least not in a way where someone has pointed it out. I do sometimes lose my train of thought, but that’s different, I think.\n\nAs far as fidgety… I mean, I’ll mess with my sleeves or move things around on my desk, but I’ve kind of always done that. Like if there’s a pen nearby I’ll click it without realizing, which apparently is annoying—my coworker once gave me one of those silent fidget things as a joke, or maybe not totally as a joke.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change like moving slower or being unusually restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped doing any of the activities you used to enjoy, like baking or stargazing, because they no longer feel worthwhile or you don’t expect to get anything out of them?\n\nPatient: Not completely, I guess. It’s more like they’ve… faded out.\n\nI still get my lab work done, which is funny because at work if there’s a procedure and samples waiting, I can sort of just follow the track. But the grant drafts or even normal project decisions take me way longer than they should. I’ll open the document and then somehow spend twenty minutes reorganizing folders or checking some detail that probably doesn’t matter.\n\nAt home, though, yeah, the baking and the bonsai stuff and the telescope plans have mostly stopped being part of my week. I keep thinking I’ll get back to them—like I still have the flour containers and the little pruning shears, and the telescope is literally right there—but I don’t plan around them anymore the way I used to. They used to kind of structure my downtime, and now I just… don’t follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood ever improves, even temporarily, in response to something positive happening, like good news or seeing someone you care about?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah, if something nice happens I can sort of respond to it—I’m not completely flat. Like if someone at the lab says something funny, I’ll laugh, or if a conversation goes well I can feel a little better for a bit.\n\nBut most days there’s still this low, gray mood kind of running underneath everything. It’s like background noise. I can still go in, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t really get that light feeling or excitement about things I used to, like baking or even just checking the telescope. I’ll think, “Oh, that should be nice,” and then it doesn’t really lift much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I don’t think it’s… nothing, exactly. I mean, sometimes I still get a little satisfaction out of small things, like if something turns out the way it’s supposed to. Though then I start thinking about whether I did it efficiently enough, which is probably not what you asked.

The “used to enjoy” part is harder. I’ll look at something and know I used to like it, but I don’t always feel pulled toward it now. But I wouldn’t say absolutely nothing brings any pleasure anymore. It’s more inconsistent, I guess.

Like when my laptop has too many tabs open and the fan starts making that little airplane noise—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nBut no, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this low, gray feeling, fatigue, and loss of enjoyment—in most days?\n\nPatient: I think… a little over two years? Not immediately like flipping a switch, but shortly after that, things just started feeling more gray as the default.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the house, honestly—old wiring, weird drafts, always some repair project, and I’d get distracted by one thing after another. But looking back, that’s around when this low baseline really settled in. Most days since then, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling more hopeless about the future, or like you're not good enough, more often lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like a dramatic “nothing will ever get better” thing, but I do catch myself assuming the house is just never going to really be settled. Like there will always be another repair, another half-finished corner, some random draft I can’t track down.\n\nAnd then that kind of turns into, “Maybe this is just how I function now,” like I’m going to keep running at this reduced level. Which sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it does make me avoid planning things ahead. Even stuff I used to like, like trips or little stargazing projects—I’ll start thinking about equipment or weather or where to go, and then I just sort of decide it probably won’t happen anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you're more fidgety or restless than usual, even when you're not particularly anxious?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed my speech being slower, at least not in a way where someone has pointed it out. I do sometimes lose my train of thought, but that’s different, I think.\n\nAs far as fidgety… I mean, I’ll mess with my sleeves or move things around on my desk, but I’ve kind of always done that. Like if there’s a pen nearby I’ll click it without realizing, which apparently is annoying—my coworker once gave me one of those silent fidget things as a joke, or maybe not totally as a joke.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change like moving slower or being unusually restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped doing any of the activities you used to enjoy, like baking or stargazing, because they no longer feel worthwhile or you don’t expect to get anything out of them?\n\nPatient: Not completely, I guess. It’s more like they’ve… faded out.\n\nI still get my lab work done, which is funny because at work if there’s a procedure and samples waiting, I can sort of just follow the track. But the grant drafts or even normal project decisions take me way longer than they should. I’ll open the document and then somehow spend twenty minutes reorganizing folders or checking some detail that probably doesn’t matter.\n\nAt home, though, yeah, the baking and the bonsai stuff and the telescope plans have mostly stopped being part of my week. I keep thinking I’ll get back to them—like I still have the flour containers and the little pruning shears, and the telescope is literally right there—but I don’t plan around them anymore the way I used to. They used to kind of structure my downtime, and now I just… don’t follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood ever improves, even temporarily, in response to something positive happening, like good news or seeing someone you care about?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah, if something nice happens I can sort of respond to it—I’m not completely flat. Like if someone at the lab says something funny, I’ll laugh, or if a conversation goes well I can feel a little better for a bit.\n\nBut most days there’s still this low, gray mood kind of running underneath everything. It’s like background noise. I can still go in, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t really get that light feeling or excitement about things I used to, like baking or even just checking the telescope. I’ll think, “Oh, that should be nice,” and then it doesn’t really lift much.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that nothing you do brings you any pleasure or satisfaction anymore, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s… nothing, exactly. I mean, sometimes I still get a little satisfaction out of small things, like if something turns out the way it’s supposed to. Though then I start thinking about whether I did it efficiently enough, which is probably not what you asked.\n\nThe “used to enjoy” part is harder. I’ll look at something and know I used to like it, but I don’t always feel pulled toward it now. But I wouldn’t say absolutely nothing brings any pleasure anymore. It’s more inconsistent, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I get through work, and then there’s the house clutter and all the little things piling up—mail, dishes, laundry, I don’t know why it all multiplies—and by the time I deal with that, I don’t really have much left for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s not like I’m crying all day or can’t function, exactly. I still go to the lab, I talk to people, I do what I’m supposed to do. But most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s weird, because I used to get a little spark from things—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing if the sky was clear enough—and now it’s more like… I do them, or I think about doing them, but it doesn’t feel light the way it used to. It’s just sort of there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It’s not dramatic, but things that used to feel kind of clean and step-by-step now take me longer. Like revising a paragraph for a grant—I used to be able to just move through it, fix the structure, tighten it up. Now I’ll stare at one sentence and change a word back and forth, then wonder if the whole point is wrong.\n\nAnd even little decisions, like picking materials for something at home—there was a repair I was trying to plan, and I kept comparing options way longer than I needed to. I don’t know, my mind just feels slower, and I second-guess small things more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like baking or using your telescope?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I still sort of… do them sometimes, or I think about doing them. But it doesn’t feel the same. Baking used to feel like a nice little project, like there was some comfort in it, and the telescope thing—checking the sky, seeing if it was clear—that used to give me this little bit of excitement.\n\nNow most days there’s just this low, gray feeling sitting in the background. I can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through things, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things the way I used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level or do you feel more fatigued than usual, even after resting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel worn down a lot. Even if I sleep through the night, I don’t really wake up feeling refreshed. It’s more like I’m starting the day already partly used up.\n\nAnd then by the time I’ve gone to work and dealt with the normal house stuff—just clutter, dishes, things sitting where they shouldn’t be—I don’t have much left. It’s funny, because sometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should clear off that table,” and then I end up noticing some random stack of papers from months ago, and suddenly it feels like this whole archaeological dig in my own house.\n\nBut, yeah, the main thing is I’m tired most of the time. Not completely unable to function, but just… worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… not really that I’ve noticed. My appetite kind of varies day to day, but I don’t think there’s been some big change.\n\nWeight-wise, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. I mean, I have a scale somewhere, but it’s probably under a pile of things in the bathroom closet, which is… its own situation. But my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think anything significant has happened there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty about things than usual, or like you’re not measuring up in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in some big, dramatic way, I don’t think. I mean, I have the usual kind of “ugh, I should be doing more” feeling, especially when I see things piling up or I think about stuff I meant to finish.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s guilt exactly. Sometimes it’s more like this vague sense that everyone else has a cleaner system for being a person, if that makes sense. Like they have their calendar and their meal plan and their laundry somehow not becoming a geological formation.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess… I haven’t really noticed feeling unusually guilty, no. More just frustrated with myself sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still more often lately?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed myself moving especially slowly, at least not in a way someone else would point out.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to tell because I’ll sit down to do one thing and then remember three other things, like I’ll get up to check the laundry and end up reorganizing one shelf for no good reason. But that’s kind of just… how my house stuff goes.\n\nSo I guess no, not really a clear change like that. I haven’t been thinking, “Wow, I can’t sit still,” or “I’m moving really slow.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of making plans to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I get kind of tired of everything being such a slog, like even small things feel weirdly complicated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been thinking life isn’t worth living. It’s more like… I wish I could hit pause for a while, if that makes sense. Like when my laptop has too many tabs open and the fan starts making that little airplane noise—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nBut no, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this low, gray feeling, fatigue, and loss of enjoyment—in most days?\n\nPatient: I think… a little over two years? It kind of started after we moved into the older house. Not immediately like flipping a switch, but shortly after that, things just started feeling more gray as the default.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the house, honestly—old wiring, weird drafts, always some repair project, and I’d get distracted by one thing after another. But looking back, that’s around when this low baseline really settled in. Most days since then, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling more hopeless about the future, or like you're not good enough, more often lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like a dramatic “nothing will ever get better” thing, but I do catch myself assuming the house is just never going to really be settled. Like there will always be another repair, another half-finished corner, some random draft I can’t track down.\n\nAnd then that kind of turns into, “Maybe this is just how I function now,” like I’m going to keep running at this reduced level. Which sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it does make me avoid planning things ahead. Even stuff I used to like, like trips or little stargazing projects—I’ll start thinking about equipment or weather or where to go, and then I just sort of decide it probably won’t happen anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you're more fidgety or restless than usual, even when you're not particularly anxious?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed my speech being slower, at least not in a way where someone has pointed it out. I do sometimes lose my train of thought, but that’s different, I think.\n\nAs far as fidgety… I mean, I’ll mess with my sleeves or move things around on my desk, but I’ve kind of always done that. Like if there’s a pen nearby I’ll click it without realizing, which apparently is annoying—my coworker once gave me one of those silent fidget things as a joke, or maybe not totally as a joke.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change like moving slower or being unusually restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped doing any of the activities you used to enjoy, like baking or stargazing, because they no longer feel worthwhile or you don’t expect to get anything out of them?\n\nPatient: Not completely, I guess. It’s more like they’ve… faded out.\n\nI still get my lab work done, which is funny because at work if there’s a procedure and samples waiting, I can sort of just follow the track. But the grant drafts or even normal project decisions take me way longer than they should. I’ll open the document and then somehow spend twenty minutes reorganizing folders or checking some detail that probably doesn’t matter.\n\nAt home, though, yeah, the baking and the bonsai stuff and the telescope plans have mostly stopped being part of my week. I keep thinking I’ll get back to them—like I still have the flour containers and the little pruning shears, and the telescope is literally right there—but I don’t plan around them anymore the way I used to. They used to kind of structure my downtime, and now I just… don’t follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood ever improves, even temporarily, in response to something positive happening, like good news or seeing someone you care about?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah, if something nice happens I can sort of respond to it—I’m not completely flat. Like if someone at the lab says something funny, I’ll laugh, or if a conversation goes well I can feel a little better for a bit.\n\nBut most days there’s still this low, gray mood kind of running underneath everything. It’s like background noise. I can still go in, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t really get that light feeling or excitement about things I used to, like baking or even just checking the telescope. I’ll think, “Oh, that should be nice,” and then it doesn’t really lift much.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that nothing you do brings you any pleasure or satisfaction anymore, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s… nothing, exactly. I mean, sometimes I still get a little satisfaction out of small things, like if something turns out the way it’s supposed to. Though then I start thinking about whether I did it efficiently enough, which is probably not what you asked.\n\nThe “used to enjoy” part is harder. I’ll look at something and know I used to like it, but I don’t always feel pulled toward it now. But I wouldn’t say absolutely nothing brings any pleasure anymore. It’s more inconsistent, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of the time, nothing you do feels satisfying or pleasurable anymore, even things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the time nothing feels satisfying. That sounds too absolute.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll start something thinking maybe it’ll feel good, and then I get sidetracked or it feels flatter than I expected. But there are still little moments where something feels okay, or I’m glad I did it. Like, not a big “this is wonderful” feeling, but not nothing either.\n\nSorry, I keep trying to be precise about the wording—“most of the time” is hard for me to pin down.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "candidates": ["F32.1", "F32.2", "F32.8"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

I can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through things, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things the way I used to. Not completely unable to function, but just… worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… not really that I’ve noticed. Sometimes it’s more like this vague sense that everyone else has a cleaner system for being a person, if that makes sense. I guess… I haven’t really noticed feeling unusually guilty, no. More just frustrated with myself sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still more often lately?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed myself moving especially slowly, at least not in a way someone else would point out.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to tell because I’ll sit down to do one thing and then remember three other things, like I’ll get up to check the laundry and end up reorganizing one shelf for no good reason. But that’s kind of just… how my house stuff goes.\n\nSo I guess no, not really a clear change like that. I haven’t been thinking, “Wow, I can’t sit still,” or “I’m moving really slow.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of making plans to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I get kind of tired of everything being such a slog, like even small things feel weirdly complicated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been thinking life isn’t worth living. It’s more like… I wish I could hit pause for a while, if that makes sense. Like when my laptop has too many tabs open and the fan starts making that little airplane noise—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nBut no, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this low, gray feeling, fatigue, and loss of enjoyment—in most days?\n\nPatient: I think… a little over two years? It kind of started after we moved into the older house. Not immediately like flipping a switch, but shortly after that, things just started feeling more gray as the default.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the house, honestly—old wiring, weird drafts, always some repair project, and I’d get distracted by one thing after another. But looking back, that’s around when this low baseline really settled in. Most days since then, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling more hopeless about the future, or like you're not good enough, more often lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like a dramatic “nothing will ever get better” thing, but I do catch myself assuming the house is just never going to really be settled. Like there will always be another repair, another half-finished corner, some random draft I can’t track down.\n\nAnd then that kind of turns into, “Maybe this is just how I function now,” like I’m going to keep running at this reduced level. Which sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it does make me avoid planning things ahead. Even stuff I used to like, like trips or little stargazing projects—I’ll start thinking about equipment or weather or where to go, and then I just sort of decide it probably won’t happen anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you're more fidgety or restless than usual, even when you're not particularly anxious?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed my speech being slower, at least not in a way where someone has pointed it out. I do sometimes lose my train of thought, but that’s different, I think.\n\nAs far as fidgety… I mean, I’ll mess with my sleeves or move things around on my desk, but I’ve kind of always done that. Like if there’s a pen nearby I’ll click it without realizing, which apparently is annoying—my coworker once gave me one of those silent fidget things as a joke, or maybe not totally as a joke.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change like moving slower or being unusually restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped doing any of the activities you used to enjoy, like baking or stargazing, because they no longer feel worthwhile or you don’t expect to get anything out of them?\n\nPatient: Not completely, I guess. It’s more like they’ve… faded out.\n\nI still get my lab work done, which is funny because at work if there’s a procedure and samples waiting, I can sort of just follow the track. But the grant drafts or even normal project decisions take me way longer than they should. I’ll open the document and then somehow spend twenty minutes reorganizing folders or checking some detail that probably doesn’t matter.\n\nAt home, though, yeah, the baking and the bonsai stuff and the telescope plans have mostly stopped being part of my week. They used to kind of structure my downtime, and now I just… don’t follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood ever improves, even temporarily, in response to something positive happening, like good news or seeing someone you care about?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah, if something nice happens I can sort of respond to it—I’m not completely flat. I get through work, and then there’s the house clutter and all the little things piling up—mail, dishes, laundry, I don’t know why it all multiplies—and by the time I deal with that, I don’t really have much left for anything else.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed feeling down or depressed most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. But most days there’s this kind of low, gray feeling in the background.\n\nIt’s weird, because I used to get a little spark from things—like baking, or checking the telescope, even just seeing if the sky was clear enough—and now it’s more like… I do them, or I think about doing them, but it doesn’t feel light the way it used to. It’s just sort of there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to think clearly, concentrate, or make decisions lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I have. It’s not dramatic, but things that used to feel kind of clean and step-by-step now take me longer. Like revising a paragraph for a grant—I used to be able to just move through it, fix the structure, tighten it up. Now I’ll stare at one sentence and change a word back and forth, then wonder if the whole point is wrong.\n\nAnd even little decisions, like picking materials for something at home—there was a repair I was trying to plan, and I kept comparing options way longer than I needed to. I don’t know, my mind just feels slower, and I second-guess small things more than I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, like baking or using your telescope?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I still sort of… do them sometimes, or I think about doing them. But it doesn’t feel the same. Baking used to feel like a nice little project, like there was some comfort in it, and the telescope thing—checking the sky, seeing if it was clear—that used to give me this little bit of excitement.\n\nNow most days there’s just this low, gray feeling sitting in the background. I can still go to the lab and talk to people and get through things, but I don’t really feel light or excited about those things the way I used to. It’s more like I’m going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a change in your energy level or do you feel more fatigued than usual, even after resting?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do feel worn down a lot. Even if I sleep through the night, I don’t really wake up feeling refreshed. It’s more like I’m starting the day already partly used up.\n\nAnd then by the time I’ve gone to work and dealt with the normal house stuff—just clutter, dishes, things sitting where they shouldn’t be—I don’t have much left. It’s funny, because sometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I should clear off that table,” and then I end up noticing some random stack of papers from months ago, and suddenly it feels like this whole archaeological dig in my own house.\n\nBut, yeah, the main thing is I’m tired most of the time. Not completely unable to function, but just… worn down.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your appetite or weight recently?\n\nPatient: Um… not really that I’ve noticed. My appetite kind of varies day to day, but I don’t think there’s been some big change.\n\nWeight-wise, I haven’t been tracking it carefully. I mean, I have a scale somewhere, but it’s probably under a pile of things in the bathroom closet, which is… its own situation. But my clothes fit about the same, so I don’t think anything significant has happened there.\n\nDoctor: Have you found yourself feeling more guilty about things than usual, or like you’re not measuring up in some way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not in some big, dramatic way, I don’t think. I mean, I have the usual kind of “ugh, I should be doing more” feeling, especially when I see things piling up or I think about stuff I meant to finish.\n\nBut I’m not sure it’s guilt exactly. Sometimes it’s more like this vague sense that everyone else has a cleaner system for being a person, if that makes sense. Like they have their calendar and their meal plan and their laundry somehow not becoming a geological formation.\n\nSorry, I’m wandering. I guess… I haven’t really noticed feeling unusually guilty, no. More just frustrated with myself sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual, or maybe feeling restless and unable to sit still more often lately?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I don’t think I’ve noticed myself moving especially slowly, at least not in a way someone else would point out.\n\nRestless… maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to tell because I’ll sit down to do one thing and then remember three other things, like I’ll get up to check the laundry and end up reorganizing one shelf for no good reason. But that’s kind of just… how my house stuff goes.\n\nSo I guess no, not really a clear change like that. I haven’t been thinking, “Wow, I can’t sit still,” or “I’m moving really slow.”\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or that life isn’t worth living?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. Not in the sense of making plans to hurt myself or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I get kind of tired of everything being such a slog, like even small things feel weirdly complicated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been thinking life isn’t worth living. It’s more like… I wish I could hit pause for a while, if that makes sense. Like when my laptop has too many tabs open and the fan starts making that little airplane noise—sorry, that’s probably not the point.\n\nBut no, I haven’t had thoughts about hurting myself.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way—this low, gray feeling, fatigue, and loss of enjoyment—in most days?\n\nPatient: I think… a little over two years? It kind of started after we moved into the older house. Not immediately like flipping a switch, but shortly after that, things just started feeling more gray as the default.\n\nAt first I thought it was just the house, honestly—old wiring, weird drafts, always some repair project, and I’d get distracted by one thing after another. But looking back, that’s around when this low baseline really settled in. Most days since then, I’d say.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling more hopeless about the future, or like you're not good enough, more often lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Not like a dramatic “nothing will ever get better” thing, but I do catch myself assuming the house is just never going to really be settled. Like there will always be another repair, another half-finished corner, some random draft I can’t track down.\n\nAnd then that kind of turns into, “Maybe this is just how I function now,” like I’m going to keep running at this reduced level. Which sounds a little silly when I say it out loud, but it does make me avoid planning things ahead. Even stuff I used to like, like trips or little stargazing projects—I’ll start thinking about equipment or weather or where to go, and then I just sort of decide it probably won’t happen anyway.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your movements or speech have slowed down, or that you're more fidgety or restless than usual, even when you're not particularly anxious?\n\nPatient: Hmm… I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed my speech being slower, at least not in a way where someone has pointed it out. I do sometimes lose my train of thought, but that’s different, I think.\n\nAs far as fidgety… I mean, I’ll mess with my sleeves or move things around on my desk, but I’ve kind of always done that. Like if there’s a pen nearby I’ll click it without realizing, which apparently is annoying—my coworker once gave me one of those silent fidget things as a joke, or maybe not totally as a joke.\n\nBut no, I don’t think I’ve noticed a clear change like moving slower or being unusually restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you completely stopped doing any of the activities you used to enjoy, like baking or stargazing, because they no longer feel worthwhile or you don’t expect to get anything out of them?\n\nPatient: Not completely, I guess. It’s more like they’ve… faded out.\n\nI still get my lab work done, which is funny because at work if there’s a procedure and samples waiting, I can sort of just follow the track. But the grant drafts or even normal project decisions take me way longer than they should. I’ll open the document and then somehow spend twenty minutes reorganizing folders or checking some detail that probably doesn’t matter.\n\nAt home, though, yeah, the baking and the bonsai stuff and the telescope plans have mostly stopped being part of my week. I keep thinking I’ll get back to them—like I still have the flour containers and the little pruning shears, and the telescope is literally right there—but I don’t plan around them anymore the way I used to. They used to kind of structure my downtime, and now I just… don’t follow through.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that your mood ever improves, even temporarily, in response to something positive happening, like good news or seeing someone you care about?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah, if something nice happens I can sort of respond to it—I’m not completely flat. Like if someone at the lab says something funny, I’ll laugh, or if a conversation goes well I can feel a little better for a bit.\n\nBut most days there’s still this low, gray mood kind of running underneath everything. It’s like background noise. I can still go in, talk to people, do what I’m supposed to do, but I don’t really get that light feeling or excitement about things I used to, like baking or even just checking the telescope. I’ll think, “Oh, that should be nice,” and then it doesn’t really lift much.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that nothing you do brings you any pleasure or satisfaction anymore, even things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s… nothing, exactly. I mean, sometimes I still get a little satisfaction out of small things, like if something turns out the way it’s supposed to. Though then I start thinking about whether I did it efficiently enough, which is probably not what you asked.\n\nThe “used to enjoy” part is harder. I’ll look at something and know I used to like it, but I don’t always feel pulled toward it now. But I wouldn’t say absolutely nothing brings any pleasure anymore. It’s more inconsistent, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that most of the time, nothing you do feels satisfying or pleasurable anymore, even things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say most of the time nothing feels satisfying. That sounds too absolute.\n\nIt’s more like… I’ll start something thinking maybe it’ll feel good, and then I get sidetracked or it feels flatter than I expected. But there are still little moments where something feels okay, or I’m glad I did it. Like, not a big “this is wonderful” feeling, but not nothing either.\n\nSorry, I keep trying to be precise about the wording—“most of the time” is hard for me to pin down.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you have completely lost interest in most activities, even ones you used to care about, and that nothing really feels rewarding or worth doing anymore?\n\nPatient: Completely lost interest… I don’t know. I don’t think I’d put it that strongly, but I’m also not sure where the line is.\n\nLike, “worth doing” is tricky because I’ll still do things if they need doing, and sometimes I’m glad after. But it’s not like I’m walking around thinking everything is pointless. It’s more muted, I guess.\n\nSorry, I’m getting caught on the wording again. “Completely” feels too big. I don’t think it’s nothing rewarding anymore, no.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you've lost interest in almost all activities, even those you used to care about, most days over the past few weeks?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “almost all,” no. I mean, I hear that and I start mentally making a list, which probably isn’t helpful, but there are still some things I’ll do and think, okay, that was decent.\n\nMost days over the past few weeks… maybe I’ve been less drawn to things, but I don’t know that I’ve lost interest across the board. It’s more like I drift away from things or don’t get as much out of them, but “almost all activities” feels too broad to me."
